3.9★ from 475 reviews. No website on their Google Maps listing; No Instagram/Facebook/TikTok linked anywhere on Google or their site; 65 one-star reviews out of 475 (14%): reputation/review replies needed; Rating 3.9: below the 4.0 people filter by.

    Or: “Would it be a bad idea if I sent you the free audit on WhatsApp? It takes 2 minutes to read.”
  7. If they say “Those bad reviews are from competitors.” → “Some may be. A calm, public reply still shows the next reader you care. I'll write them for you. Can I send them?”
  8. Future pace “Every review has a calm owner's reply, the rating climbs, and new members come in from Maps.”

No business matches that search.

Belfort's competitor-insight opener is one tactic. Each call sheet below combines it with the other methods that hold up, listed here with how strong the evidence is.

TacticWhere it comes fromHow strongHow you use it
State the reason for the call earlyGong (secondary summaries report about 2.1× success; I couldn't find the figure on Gong's own pages)Medium"The reason I'm calling is…" inside the first 10 seconds
Permission-based openerGong, 300M calls: 11.18% success vs a 1.5% baselineStrong (large dataset, vendor-run)"Can I take 30 seconds to explain why I called?"
Don't open with "Did I catch you at a bad time?"Gong: about 2% success, the weakest opener testedStrongNever use it, even though it sounds polite
Competitor or market insight firstBelfort (Straight Line); also the "Challenger" approachExpert methodOne true, public fact about a nearby competitor
Poke-the-bear questionJosh BraunExpert methodA neutral question that lets them discover the gap themselves, instead of you pitching it
Problem before solution, no ROI promisesGong: ROI language lowered cold-email success by about 15%Medium (email data)Talk about their missing link, not "I'll grow your revenue X%"
Three Tens (certainty in idea, you, plan)BelfortExpert methodThe fact (idea) → one real result (you) → a small free first step (plan)
No-oriented askChris Voss, Never Split the DifferenceExpert method"Would it be a bad idea if I sent you the audit?" Saying no feels safe, and here no means yes
Interest ask in messagesGong: an interest CTA got about 30% vs about 15% for a specific meeting ask (cold email)MediumEnd WhatsApp and DMs with "Open to seeing it?", not "Can we meet Tuesday at 3?"
Give first (reciprocity)Cialdini, InfluenceStrong (decades of research)The free audit, or 3 free images
Looping, at most twiceBelfortExpert method (shortened)Acknowledge → one new fact → ask the small thing again
Future pacingBelfort / NLP sellingExpert methodOne sentence picturing their customer once it's fixed
Warm, calm toneVoss; GongStrongA friendly voice beats a perfect script; speak your own Egyptian Arabic

Why would they talk to a 19-year-old freelancer?

Most of them won't, if you sell "marketing management". That's what agencies with teams sell. Some will, if you bring:

  1. A specific broken thing they didn't know about (the Google Form, the Yellow Pages links, the wrong Instagram). Nobody cares about your age when you point at their own listing.
  2. A small fixed job with a price, not a monthly retainer: the listing fixed, a one-page site, review replies for a month, 3 product images.
  3. Proof: Grapes Holiday and Rihla are real numbers from real money.
  4. Speed and direct access: you answer WhatsApp yourself, the same day.

Who to aim at now:

  • Owner-run, single-location businesses where the owner is in the building: clinics, salons, small restaurants, padel clubs.
  • Not groups with branches, and not places whose social media already works.
  • After 2–3 local case studies with before/after numbers, go back to the parked ones.

Your age and size:

  • Don't lead with your age, and don't hide it if asked. "I'm 19, and here's what I did for Grapes" is fine.
  • Don't say "we" as if you had a team you don't have.

Getting past the order line, the right way. I won't dig up managers' personal mobile numbers. Calling a private number nobody gave you is exactly what makes you a cold caller, and it can get you blocked or reported. Use the routes the business itself publishes:

  1. Ask for a name, not a transfer. "Who's the manager, and when is he usually in?" Then call back and ask for him by name. A name changes how the next person treats you.
  2. The Instagram or Facebook DM. In restaurants and salons, the owner or the marketing person reads it, not the order-taker.
  3. The business email or "for business" number in their Instagram bio, Facebook "About" page or website. They published it for exactly this.
  4. LinkedIn. Search "[business] owner" or "marketing manager" and send a short connection note with the one fact.
  5. Walk in at a quiet hour (3–5 PM for restaurants, late morning for salons and gyms) and ask for the manager. This works best for places near you: Zayed, October, Hadayek, Haram.
  6. Mutual contacts. Ask friends and GUC classmates whether anyone knows the owner.

The call in order (each sheet follows it):

  1. Permission + reason (first 4–10 seconds): who you are, why you're calling, 30 seconds?
  2. Insight: the competitor fact, short.
  3. Poke: one neutral question about the gap. Then stop talking.
  4. Qualify: 2–3 questions, and listen.
  5. Certainty: one real result.
  6. No-oriented small ask: the free audit.
  7. Loop once or twice on an objection, then let it go.
  8. Future pace, then get the name, the WhatsApp and the follow-up day.

What I left out of Belfort, on purpose:

  • Pressure, looping 3–5 times, fake urgency, closing on the first call, and "say whatever closes".
  • He was convicted of fraud, so keep his structure and leave his ethics.
  • A second "no" ends the call politely. Note a follow-up for 3 months later.

Rules:

  • Use only true, public facts (Google Maps, their own posts), and check them on your phone before each call.
  • Never insult the competitor, and never say a competitor is your client.
  • If they ask how you know: "It's public on Google Maps. I compared the listings in your area."
  • Dubai: message first. Call only after they reply.

Sources:

  • Gong, best and worst cold call openers (300M calls): https://www.gong.io/blog/the-best-and-worst-cold-call-openers-backed-by-data-from-300m-calls
  • Gong, cold call opening lines (90,380 calls): https://www.gong.io/resources/labs/cold-call-opening-lines/
  • Gong, sales stats (interest CTA, ROI language): https://gong.io/blog/sales-stats
  • The "reason for my call" 2.1× figure (secondary source): https://brendonrod.substack.com/p/gong30-mind-blowing-sales-stats-that
  • Black Swan Group (Chris Voss), fixing a bad cold-calling script: https://www.blackswanltd.com/the-edge/fixing-a-bad-cold-calling-script
  • Josh Braun, Poke the Bear course: https://joshbraun.teachable.com/courses/poke/lectures/45769066
  • Belfort, The Way of the Wolf (summary): https://www.theinvestorspodcast.com/blog/the-way-of-the-wolf-straight-line-selling-book-summary/
